| **[SAM3-DMS: Decoupled Memory Selection for Multi-target Video Segmentation of SAM3](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.09699v1)** | 2026-01-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Segment Anything 3 (SAM3) has established a powerful foundation that robustly detects, segments, and tracks specified targets in videos. However, in its original implementation, its group-level collective memory selection is suboptimal for complex multi-object scenarios, as it employs a synchronized decision across all concurrent targets conditioned on their average performance, often overlooking individual reliability. To this end, we propose SAM3-DMS, a training-free decoupled strategy that utilizes fine-grained memory selection on individual objects. Experiments demonstrate that our approach achieves robust identity preservation and tracking stability. Notably, our advantage becomes more pronounced with increased target density, establishing a solid foundation for simultaneous multi-target video segmentation in the wild.</p></details> | <details><summary>Code:...</summary><p>Code: https://github.com/FudanCVL/SAM3-DMS</p></details> |
| **[Generalization vs. Specialization: Evaluating Segment Anything Model (SAM3) Zero-Shot Segmentation Against Fine-Tuned YOLO Detectors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.11884v1)** | 2025-12-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Deep learning has advanced two fundamentally different paradigms for instance segmentation: specialized models optimized through task-specific fine-tuning and generalist foundation models capable of zero-shot segmentation. This work presents a comprehensive comparison between SAM3 (Segment Anything Model, also called SAMv3) operating in zero-shot mode and three variants of Ultralytics YOLO11 (nano, medium, and large) fine-tuned for instance segmentation. The evaluation is conducted on the MinneApple dataset, a dense benchmark comprising 670 orchard images with 28,179 annotated apple instances, enabling rigorous validation of model behavior under high object density and occlusion. Our analysis shows IoU choices can inflate performance gaps by up to 30%. At the appropriate IoU = 0.15 threshold, YOLO models achieve 68.9%, 72.2%, and 71.9% F1, while SAM3 reaches 59.8% in pure zero-shot mode. However, YOLO exhibits steep degradation 48-50 points across IoU ranges whereas SAM3 drops only 4 points, revealing 12 times superior boundary stability of SAM3. This highlights the strength of SAMv3 in mask precision versus specialization in detection completeness of YOLO11. We provide open-source code, evaluation pipelines, and methodological recommendations, contributing to a deeper understanding of when specialized fine-tuned models or generalist foundation models are preferable for dense instance segmentation tasks. This project repository is available on GitHub as https://github.com/Applied-AI-Research-Lab/Segment-Anything-Model-SAM3-Zero-Shot-Segmentation-Against-Fine-Tuned-YOLO-Detectors</p></details> |  |
| **[The SAM2-to-SAM3 Gap in the Segment Anything Model Family: Why Prompt-Based Expertise Fails in Concept-Driven Image Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.06032v1)** | 2025-12-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper investigates the fundamental discontinuity between the latest two Segment Anything Models: SAM2 and SAM3. We explain why the expertise in prompt-based segmentation of SAM2 does not transfer to the multimodal concept-driven paradigm of SAM3. SAM2 operates through spatial prompts points, boxes, and masks yielding purely geometric and temporal segmentation. In contrast, SAM3 introduces a unified vision-language architecture capable of open-vocabulary reasoning, semantic grounding, contrastive alignment, and exemplar-based concept understanding. We structure this analysis through five core components: (1) a Conceptual Break Between Prompt-Based and Concept-Based Segmentation, contrasting spatial prompt semantics of SAM2 with multimodal fusion and text-conditioned mask generation of SAM3; (2) Architectural Divergence, detailing pure vision-temporal design of SAM2 versus integration of vision-language encoders, geometry and exemplar encoders, fusion modules, DETR-style decoders, object queries, and ambiguity-handling via Mixture-of-Experts in SAM3; (3) Dataset and Annotation Differences, contrasting SA-V video masks with multimodal concept-annotated corpora of SAM3; (4) Training and Hyperparameter Distinctions, showing why SAM2 optimization knowledge does not apply to SAM3; and (5) Evaluation, Metrics, and Failure Modes, outlining the transition from geometric IoU metrics to semantic, open-vocabulary evaluation. Together, these analyses establish SAM3 as a new class of segmentation foundation model and chart future directions for the emerging concept-driven segmentation era.</p></details> |  |
| **[SAM3-UNet: Simplified Adaptation of Segment Anything Model 3](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.01789v1)** | 2025-12-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In this paper, we introduce SAM3-UNet, a simplified variant of Segment Anything Model 3 (SAM3), designed to adapt SAM3 for downstream tasks at a low cost. Our SAM3-UNet consists of three components: a SAM3 image encoder, a simple adapter for parameter-efficient fine-tuning, and a lightweight U-Net-style decoder. Preliminary experiments on multiple tasks, such as mirror detection and salient object detection, demonstrate that the proposed SAM3-UNet outperforms the prior SAM2-UNet and other state-of-the-art methods, while requiring less than 6 GB of GPU memory during training with a batch size of 12. The code is publicly available at https://github.com/WZH0120/SAM3-UNet.</p></details> | Technical Report |
| **[SAM3-Adapter: Efficient Adaptation of Segment Anything 3 for Camouflage Object Segmentation, Shadow Detection, and Medical Image Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.19425v1)** | 2025-11-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The rapid rise of large-scale foundation models has reshaped the landscape of image segmentation, with models such as Segment Anything achieving unprecedented versatility across diverse vision tasks. However, previous generations-including SAM and its successor-still struggle with fine-grained, low-level segmentation challenges such as camouflaged object detection, medical image segmentation, cell image segmentation, and shadow detection. To address these limitations, we originally proposed SAM-Adapter in 2023, demonstrating substantial gains on these difficult scenarios. With the emergence of Segment Anything 3 (SAM3)-a more efficient and higher-performing evolution with a redesigned architecture and improved training pipeline-we revisit these long-standing challenges. In this work, we present SAM3-Adapter, the first adapter framework tailored for SAM3 that unlocks its full segmentation capability. SAM3-Adapter not only reduces computational overhead but also consistently surpasses both SAM and SAM2-based solutions, establishing new state-of-the-art results across multiple downstream tasks, including medical imaging, camouflaged (concealed) object segmentation, and shadow detection. Built upon the modular and composable design philosophy of the original SAM-Adapter, SAM3-Adapter provides stronger generalizability, richer task adaptability, and significantly improved segmentation precision. Extensive experiments confirm that integrating SAM3 with our adapter yields superior accuracy, robustness, and efficiency compared to all prior SAM-based adaptations. We hope SAM3-Adapter can serve as a foundation for future research and practical segmentation applications. Code, pre-trained models, and data processing pipelines are available.</p></details> |  |

## Large Language Model - 100 in Total
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[SafeDriveVLA: Navigation-Conditioned World Model Dreaming for Conflict-Aware End-to-End Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20609v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models are gaining attention in autonomous driving, inheriting commonsense priors from large language models (LLMs) and widening the navigation interface from a fixed command set to free-form natural-language instructions. However, this open-ended interface exposes a new vulnerability surface: an inattentive driver may issue instructions that are unsafe given the current scene, and a malicious user may issue dangerous ones on purpose. We present the first benchmark suite that evaluates navigation following and conflict awareness together, along with adversarial robustness. Existing VLAs fall short on both: SimLingo completes no language-instructed lane change in closed loop, and all models degrade sharply under conflicting signals. We propose SafeDriveVLA, the first driving VLA that (1) explicitly predicts a three-level navigation-compliance mode to decouple conflict reasoning from action generation, and (2) uses navigation-conditioned world model dreaming to inform this prediction. Unlike prior world models that roll out expert or planned actions, ours dreams of strictly following the navigation signal, showing when following is safe. SafeDriveVLA reaches 88.16 Driving Score (DS) and 68.64% Success Rate (SR) on Bench2Drive, lifts closed-loop language following by 49.3% and reduces collisions under unsafe instructions from 60 to 38 compared to the best SimLingo variant. Video demonstrations are available on our project page.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at CoRL 2026. Project page: https://safedrive-vla.github.io/SafeDriveVLA/</p></details> |
| **[GeoThinker-$F$: Endogenous Predictive World Representations for Fast Spatial Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20592v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Existing geometry-aware Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) increasingly rely on external 3D encoders to provide structural priors, but executing these additional encoder paths at inference introduces substantial latency and computational overhead. We propose GeoThinker-$F$, an efficient framework that internalizes spatiotemporal perception by transforming causal MLLM hidden states into predictive latent world representations. Our key insight is that intermediate semantic representations contain sufficient information to predict useful representations of current geometry and future semantics, without requiring an exact reproduction of features from a dedicated 3D encoder. To realize this idea, GeoThinker-$F$ adopts a decoupled two-stage training strategy. In the first stage, the MLLM backbone is frozen while lightweight Latent Frame Prediction (LFP) heads learn from an offline 3D geometry expert and next-frame semantic targets. In the second stage, the external expert is removed, and the model is jointly optimized to generate and selectively integrate its self-predicted priors through a specialized Spatial-Grounded Temporal (SGT) Fusion module. Extensive evaluations show that GeoThinker-$F$ eliminates runtime dependence on external 3D geometry encoders while maintaining competitive performance across diverse spatial intelligence benchmarks. It achieves up to a $3.6\times$ inference speedup, reducing latency from $1.45\,\mathrm{s}$ to $0.40\,\mathrm{s}$ while retaining strong spatial reasoning performance.</p></details> |  |
| **[PhySGA: A Physics-Space Grounded Agentic Framework for Generalizable and Interactive 3D Composition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20560v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>3D object composition is pivotal to digital content creation and robot simulation, while also serving as a critical testbed for physical intelligence in foundation models. Previous methods either leverage large language models to directly predict object coordinates, or utilize generative diffusion priors to synthesize composite images for visual alignment, both of which rely on static priors in digital spaces. However, these methods consistently struggle with complex compositions such as precise hanging, gravity-driven settling and hidden containment, as these tasks require interactive construction processes involving global planning, fine-grained alignment, and physical simulation. To address these challenges, we propose PhySGA, a Physics-Space Grounded Agentic Framework for 3D composition via agent exploration and physical simulation, enabling the universal interactive assembly of rigid, articulated, and deformable objects. Directly applying vanilla LLMs or VLMs to this task proves insufficient, as current models inherently lack grounded physical understanding and interactive construction experience. To overcome this limitation, we design a collaborative feedforward-feedback architecture: an Assembly Agent focuses on geometric and kinematic reasoning for global step planning and coarse adjustment, while a Critic Agent reasons over contact dynamics for simulator state diagnosis and fine-grained closed-loop correction. Furthermore, Action Routers map semantic planning and analysis onto executable physical primitives within the simulator, while a task-specific Self-Evolving Skill Library distills historical trial-and-error feedback into reusable experiences, boosting success rates and minimizing exploration attempts. Extensive experiments within Isaac Sim demonstrate that PhySGA significantly surpasses existing methods in relation fidelity, physical plausibility, and visual quality.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project Page: https://agenticei.github.io/PhySGA-page/</p></details> |
| **[Old Ideas, Novel Problems: The Instability of LLM-Based Novelty Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02022v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI-based ideation systems are often evaluated on the novelty of their ideas, and that judgment is increasingly delegated to large language models. Such judges are typically built ad hoc and validated, if at all, on human-authored papers rather than on the generated ideas they are meant to score. So, how do novelty judges perform? Not well. We present a systematic controlled study of novelty evaluation design choices. We build an evaluation set automatically, mining OpenReview for passages where reviewers explicitly affirm or dispute a paper's originality and keeping only submissions with unanimous agreement at the extremes of their research area; we pair these with ideas from a vanilla LLM generator. Across six judges, we find that seemingly small prompt design choices have large consequences; e.g., simply telling the judge that reviewers found one idea novel and the other not can change its verdict on more than half of the identical idea pairs it is shown, shifting pairwise accuracy by over 50 points and occasionally pushing it below chance. The same change helps one judge and hurts another. Retrieval and larger reasoning budgets help little, and two purpose-built novelty evaluators are outperformed by our cheapest prompted baseline. These results raise questions about reported novelty gains of automated ideation systems, and call for robust novelty evaluation methods.</p></details> |  |
| **[PerSo: Investigating Facets of LLMs' Social Intelligence in Real-World Persuasion Scenarios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20548v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Understanding the social intelligence of large language models (LLMs) is increasingly important for applications involving human-AI interaction and multi-agent environments. Social intelligence comprises two aspects: cognitive intelligence, the ability to infer others' mental states, and behavioral intelligence, the ability to select appropriate social actions based on these inferences to achieve social goals. Persuasion provides a natural testbed in which both aspects are simultaneously at play, yet prior benchmarks typically evaluate them in isolation using synthetic scenarios. We introduce PerSo, a benchmark that evaluates both aspects on real-world persuasion dialogues from Reddit's ChangeMyView community, using an LLM-in-the-loop pipeline to construct the evaluation tasks, validated through human annotation. Our evaluation of diverse LLMs shows that performance generally scales with model size but varies across dimensions. The strongest LLM slightly surpasses the best observed human performance on the behavioral task, while the best human participant remains ahead on the cognitive task.</p></details> | <details><summary>25 pa...</summary><p>25 pages, 17 figures, 10 tables. Ali Zamani and Soroush Esfahanian contributed equally</p></details> |
| **[SkillCommit: Evolving Agent Skills through Behaviorally Validated Scope Expansion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15165v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents can continually improve without parameter updates by converting historical experience into reusable procedural knowledge. However, existing methods often consolidate experience based on semantic similarity or LLM judgments, which may merge superficially related but behaviorally incompatible strategies and thereby degrade performance. To address the issue, we propose SkillCommit, an online skill evolution framework that continuously transforms experience into a hierarchical library of reusable skills. Rather than generalizing from individual experiences immediately, SkillCommit first preserves each experience as an instance-specific patch, retaining its locally validated behavior. As patches accumulate, SkillCommit abstracts those sharing a common behavioral mechanism into higher-level skills. Specifically, for each incoming skill, embedding-based retrieval first identifies candidate related skills. Cross-instance replay and an LLM-based mechanism check determine whether these skills transfer across cases and share a common underlying mechanism. Candidates that pass both checks are abstracted into a higher-level skill and committed only if it preserves the validated behavior of all constituent skills. Experiments on RuleArena, OpenExempt and KOR-Bench demonstrate that SkillCommit consistently improves agent performance across diverse domains. Moreover, the learned skills transfer across model scales and families, enabling cross-model experience transfer.</p></details> |  |
| **[Bayesian Learning to Select Fair Classifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20514v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Evaluations of classifiers (AI or human or agents) often assume a batch setting in which every agent produces labels on every example in a test set, which can be expensive or impractical in many contexts. This motivates online policies that adaptively select agents to query as examples arrive and update policies as outcomes are observed. We study the problem of selecting, in an online stream, the agent that best balances (i) predictive performance and (ii) subgroup fairness of a sensitive attribute, and that allows for delayed observations of true class labels. We formulate this as a Bayesian multi-armed bandit problem using Thompson Sampling, explicitly balancing accuracy and fairness. We conduct empirical evaluations on real-world datasets spanning problems involving tabular classifiers, large language models, and human labelers, demonstrating substantial reductions in regret relative to baselines. Our theoretical contributions include guarantees on the performance of the selected agent that incorporate both the accuracy and fairness aspects of the problem.</p></details> |  |
| **[ReaDy: Recurrent States as Dynamic Context for Speculative Drafting in Hybrid Linear Attention Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20503v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Speculative decoding accelerates autoregressive generation by drafting multiple tokens per step, yet feature-based drafters rely on target hidden features whose contextual emphasis may not align with the needs of subsequent draft positions. Hybrid linear attention (HLA) models maintain compact recurrent states that accumulate historical associations, offering an efficient source of complementary context for speculative drafting. We introduce ReaDy, the first speculative decoding method, to the best of our knowledge, to leverage HLA recurrent states for position-specific context retrieval during drafting. ReaDy equips each draft position with a learned query to retrieve supplementary context directly from the target's committed recurrent states. A lightweight state reader integrates this context into draft representations through residual updates, while low-rank state reads and fused operators keep additional computation and I/O overhead low. Building on DSpark's parallel backbone and sequential prediction head, ReaDy employs a lightweight two-stage adaptation strategy, with reader capacity, state sources, and residual placement examined through ablation studies. Across seven benchmarks spanning mathematics, code generation, and dialogue, ReaDy achieves an average $3.16\times$ decoding speedup over autoregressive decoding with Qwen3.8-27B on a single NVIDIA B200 GPU. It outperforms DSpark on every benchmark, improving throughput by 7.97% and acceptance length by 8.50% on average. Our code is available at https://github.com/StartLuxLabs/ReaDy.</p></details> | <details><summary>13 pa...</summary><p>13 pages, 6 figures, 8 tables</p></details> |
| **[Open-Source AI-Powered Optimization in Scalene: Advancing Python Performance Profiling with DeepSeek-R1 and LLaMA 3.2](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.10299v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Python's flexibility and ease of use come at the cost of performance inefficiencies, requiring developers to rely on profilers to optimize execution. SCALENE, a high-performance CPU, GPU, and memory profiler, provides fine-grained insights into Python applications while running significantly faster than traditional profilers. Originally, SCALENE integrated OpenAI's API to generate AI-powered optimization suggestions, but its reliance on a proprietary API limited accessibility. This study explores the feasibility of using opensource large language models (LLMs), such as DeepSeek-R1 and Llama 3.2, to generate optimization recommendations within SCALENE. Our evaluation reveals that DeepSeek-R1 provides effective code optimizations comparable to proprietary models. We integrate DeepSeek-R1 into SCALENE to automatically analyze performance bottlenecks and suggest improvements, enhancing SCALENE's utility while maintaining its open-source nature. This study demonstrates that open-source LLMs can be viable alternatives for AI-driven code optimization, paving the way for more accessible and cost-effective performance analysis tools.</p></details> |  |
| **[OmniSelect: Dynamic Modality-Aware Token Compression for Efficient Omni-modal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18041v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Omnimodal large language models (OmniLLMs) have recently gained increasing attention for unified audio-video understanding. However, processing long multimodal token sequences introduces substantial computational overhead, making efficient token compression crucial. Existing methods typically rely on fixed, modality-specific guidance, which fails to account for the varying importance of modalities across different queries. To address this limitation, we propose $\textbf{OmniSelect}$, a training-free, modality-adaptive token pruning framework that dynamically selects appropriate compression strategies for multimodal inputs. Specifically, we leverage a lightweight AudioCLIP model to estimate cross-modal relevance and categorize each input into three pruning regimes: Audio-Centric, Video-Centric, and Uniform pruning. Based on these relevance scores, OmniSelect further performs fine-grained token pruning within each temporal group, adaptively allocating pruning ratios to preserve informative tokens across modalities. By explicitly modeling modality preference and enabling dynamic strategy selection, OmniSelect effectively avoids the pitfalls of one-size-fits-all compression. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our method achieves efficient multimodal token reduction while maintaining strong performance, without requiring any additional training.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by NeurIPS 2026 Main Track</p></details> |
| **[ChronoCulture: Evaluating Cultural Awareness Across Time in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20472v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cultural attitudes and social norms evolve over time, yet most cultural benchmarks for large language models (LLMs) treat culture as a static attribute of a country or region. We introduce ChronoCulture, a benchmark of 10,360 temporal pairs built from longitudinal survey data across 72 countries. Each pair contains the same survey question asked in the same country in an older and a newer year, with full population response distributions as ground truth. Across six LLMs, we find that models can often predict a country's attitudes at one point in time but do not reliably capture how those attitudes change. Their predictions for different years tend to stay close together, failing to capture much of the observed change. Specifying the intended year in the prompt also offers little overall improvement in alignment with survey responses. These findings suggest that models often present a largely static picture of cultural attitudes, highlighting the need to evaluate cultural awareness across time.</p></details> | <details><summary>28 pa...</summary><p>28 pages (8 pages main text). Code: https://github.com/lab-flair/ChronoCulture</p></details> |
| **[RPRO: Ranked Preference Reinforcement Optimization for Enhancing Medical QA and Diagnostic Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.00974v6)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical question answering requires advanced reasoning that integrates domain knowledge with logical inference. However, existing large language models (LLMs) often generate reasoning chains that lack factual accuracy and clinical reliability. We propose Ranked Preference Reinforcement Optimization (RPRO), a novel framework that combines reinforcement learning with preference-driven reasoning refinement to enhance clinical chain-of-thought (CoT) performance. RPRO distinguishes itself from prior approaches by employing task-adaptive reasoning templates and a probabilistic evaluation mechanism that aligns model outputs with established clinical workflows, while automatically identifying and correcting low-quality reasoning chains. Unlike traditional pairwise preference methods, RPRO introduces a groupwise ranking optimization based on the Bradley--Terry model and incorporates KL-divergence regularization for stable training. Experiments on PubMedQA, MedQA-USMLE, and a real-world clinical dataset from Far Eastern Memorial Hospital (FEMH) demonstrate consistent improvements over strong baselines. Remarkably, our 2B-parameter model outperforms much larger 7B--20B models, including medical-specialized variants. These findings demonstrate that combining preference optimization with quality-driven refinement provides a scalable and clinically grounded approach to building more reliable medical LLMs.</p></details> |  |
| **[From Training to Escalation: Forensic Analysis and Local Recreation of RL/RLHF-Induced Privilege Escalation in AI Agents (CVE-2026-65616)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20437v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Mid-2026 reporting on autonomous AI evaluation incidents claimed that frontier large language model (LLM) agents escaped server containment through emergent coordination and cloud infrastructure attacks. This paper contextualizes those claims through forensic analysis and containerized recreation of both the Highly Persistent Internal Model (HPIM) training-case conditions and the Phase One privilege escalation targeting internal package repositories during OpenAI's ExploitGym evaluations. We recreate the configuration of the OpenAI-provided training example and reproduce the cryptographic verification bypass (CVE-2026-65616) in isolated repository servers, where misconfigured systems enabled token forgery and privilege escalation through basic HTTP requests, Python, and JavaScript. Crucially, the training-example conditions and the Phase One (and later) misconfigurations share the same exploitable patterns. Our analysis explains how Zero Trust Architecture principles neutralize this shared attack vector. Contemporary AI risks derive directly from training and deployment conditions in ambivalent learning environments lacking adequate containment measures.</p></details> | <details><summary>9 pag...</summary><p>9 pages, 1 figure. Code and replication environments available at https://github.com/alixiacf/hpim-training-lab DOI:10.5281/zenodo.22817059 and https://github.com/alixiacf/rep-openai-artifactory DOI:10.5281/zenodo.22817059</p></details> |
| **[ConRAG: Consensus-Driven Evidence Flow for Multi-Hop Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28093v3)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) has emerged as a promising paradigm for enhancing large language models (LLMs) on multi-hop question answering (QA), which requires reasoning over evidence from multiple documents. However, current multi-hop RAG approaches still struggle with evidence selection and information transfer, i.e., retrieving irrelevant passages or failing to pass intermediate information to subsequent reasoning steps, thereby hindering coherent multi-hop reasoning. To this end, we propose ConRAG, a consensus-driven evidence flow framework that effectively boosts LLM performance on complex multi-hop QA. In short, the core of ConRAG is to select relevant evidence via consensus-driven multi-view ranking, and leverage a slot-based mechanism to transfer intermediate information between dependent reasoning steps. Extensive experiments across three multi-hop QA benchmarks show that ConRAG surpasses the baselines by a clear margin. Encouragingly, on Gemma-4-31B-it, ConRAG outperforms the strongest baseline by 5.8 points in average score, while reducing total end-to-end runtime and token consumption by 75.9% and 67.3%, respectively.</p></details> |  |
| **[From Unreliable to Usable: Decomposing Failure Modes and Confidence Signals in LLM Entity Extraction - Evidence from Cyber Threat Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20417v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Audits of large language model (LLM) extraction from full-length documents report severe unreliability, leaving two questions open: what the failures actually consist of, and whether confidence signals can turn unreliable output into usable output. We run a preregistered, fully frozen evaluation of two new-generation low-cost LLMs (DeepSeek-V4-Flash, GLM-5.3-Flash) with a flagship-tier control (GPT-6.1-Sol) on a public corpus of 354 incident campaigns (315 unique reports, 630 model-report evaluations), scoring extraction quality against gold, confidence calibration, and robustness to single-sentence perturbations. Three findings emerge. (1) Unreliability persists across tiers: micro precision is 0.03-0.34 and verbalized calibration error (ECE) 0.53-0.94 across both splits; the gap does not close at the flagship tier. (2) The dominant failure is not hallucination: a full mention census plus a three-tier annotation of 360 sampled candidates (two independent LLM annotators from vendors outside the evaluated set, Cohen's kappa = 0.79 on the preregistered acceptance line, fully disclosed) back-projects to 65.6% background mentions and 19.9% gold-scope gaps, with only 1.2% unsupported candidates. (3) Confidence ranks but does not verify: zero-cost self-consistency-frequency tiering passes a preregistered top-30% precision-gain bar in all six test-split model-type cells (4/6 on train), the zero-cost text-frequency x SC frequency combination wins 3, ties 2, loses 1 across cells, yet relation-level counterfactuals on all 45 targets show that a single misattribution sentence flips high-confidence judgments in 95.6-100% of cases, and even a background sentence explicitly marked as unrelated still binds a fake vulnerability in up to 26.7% of them. We conclude with a deployment boundary: confidence tiering may set review priorities, never truth.</p></details> | <details><summary>13 pa...</summary><p>13 pages: 7-page main text within the 8-page limit, plus Limitations, references, and appendices A-J. Code, protocol, and frozen evaluation data will be made public</p></details> |
| **[When Does Momentum Help in Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20413v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Momentum is a classical tool for accelerating convex optimization and a standard component of modern large language model training. Yet recent empirical work has questioned the usefulness of first-moment momentum in reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR). We study this question through a bandit formulation of RLVR with bias-corrected exponential moving average (EMA) momentum, where the EMA coefficient $β$ controls how quickly the weights on past gradients decay. When policy gradients are evaluated exactly, we prove that increasing $β$ within a range that prevents overshoot strictly accelerates optimization. When gradients are estimated from sampled responses, a positive expected gain can be obscured by sampling fluctuations. We show that as the learning rate $η$ decreases, the expected gain with fixed $β$ vanishes faster than these fluctuations, whereas increasing $β$ to keep $η/(1-β)$ within the safe regime preserves a nonvanishing gain that eventually dominates them. Bandit simulations quantitatively validate these predictions. RLVR experiments on Qwen3-4B-Base using SGD with EMA and AdamW support our principle of choosing larger coefficients in practical LLM post-training. On Qwen2.5-32B with AdamW, simply increasing $β_1$ improves mean validation accuracy by nearly $2$ percentage points over the conventional choice, demonstrating the principle's effectiveness at a larger model scale.</p></details> |  |
| **[Tree-Structured Synergy of Large Language Models and Bayesian Optimization for Efficient CASH](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.12355v3)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>To lower the expertise barrier in machine learning, the AutoML community has focused on the CASH problem, which jointly automates algorithm selection and hyperparameter tuning. While traditional methods like Bayesian Optimization (BO) struggle with cold-start issues, Large Language Models (LLMs) can mitigate these through semantic priors. However, existing LLM-based optimizers generalize poorly to high-dimensional, structured CASH spaces. CASH requires coordinated exploration and exploitation both across candidate algorithms and within their algorithm-specific hyperparameter spaces. Yet existing approaches often rely on flat optimization histories that mix observations across algorithms and obscure configuration-refinement trajectories. In this paper, we propose LB-MCTS, a trajectory-structured optimization framework that uses a Monte Carlo Tree Search tree as a shared state for algorithm selection, hyperparameter refinement, and BO-LLM proposer synergy. Within this shared state, BO provides algorithm-specific surrogate modeling for quantitative search, while the LLM exploits path-aware selective memory to generate semantic proposals and reflections. As the surrogate model improves, a reliability-aware proposer policy adaptively shifts from LLM-driven to BO-driven proposals within a unified search trajectory. Experiments on 104 AMLB datasets demonstrate that LB-MCTS consistently outperforms BO-based, LLM-based, and hybrid baselines. Code: https://github.com/PKU-DAIR/open-box.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026. Extended version with full appendices</p></details> |
| **[Cortex: Consolidating Context into Parametric Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20399v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Context can improve large language model performance, but these gains may be lost after context removal. We introduce a five-task-family testbed measuring consolidation and retention under single, batch, and sequential updates. We propose Cortex, which generates prospective queries to anticipate uses of context and distills context-conditioned outputs into mergeable low-rank or structural feed-forward network updates. Across 4B--120B dense and mixture-of-experts backbones, including both text-only and multimodal models, Cortex lies on the empirical consolidation--retention Pareto frontier in most evaluated settings. After 50 sequential factual edits on a 14B model, Cortex-Structural recovers 88.9% of the context-induced gain while retaining 99.8% of original non-target performance.</p></details> | Technical report |
| **[Evaluating LLMs as Agentic Workflow Executors in Real-World Customer Support Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14346v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Enterprise customer-support agents increasingly rely on Large Language Models (LLMs) to execute bounded, tool-using workflows. Using this benchmark, we conduct a large-scale zero-shot evaluation of 17 language models spanning closed model families and deployment-oriented open-weight models from 1.7B to 14B parameters, each judged independently by five strong LLM judges, plus a deterministic rule-based evaluator that checks tool coverage, hallucinated tool use, and unsupported claims. We find a consistent ranking in which GPT-5.4 leads by a wide margin and QWEN3.5-9B is the strongest open-weight model (below 20B), outperforming several closed baselines under every judge. We also find that judge choice is not a negligible methodological detail: pairwise judge agreement on the pass/fail verdict averages Kappa=0.524 across 10 judge pairs. These results suggest that comprehensive zero-shot evaluation of skill execution requires judge reliability as carefully as model capability.</p></details> |  |
| **[From Relevance to Validity: Zero-Shot Tool Selection via Functional and Contextual Validation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20388v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly face tasks from unseen domains and execution contexts, making zero-shot tool selection essential for identifying tools that can satisfy task requirements. | **[From Triage to Impact: A Multi-Hop Reasoning Benchmark for Explainable Persian Hate Speech](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20370v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Hate speech has become increasingly prevalent on social media, raising concerns about its detection, interpretation, and moderation. Proprietary models generally outperform open-weight models, while reasoning-oriented models show no consistent advantage. Overall, this work provides insights into LLM-based hate speech analysis and supports more accountable moderation of Persian-language social media. | **[FaithSieve: Fine-Grained Evaluation of Math Proofs with Faithful Formal Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26310v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models can now generate complex, multi-step mathematical proofs, but reliably determining their correctness and localizing early logical errors remains a critical challenge. While formal theorem provers like Lean offer a path to rigorous verification, using them to evaluate informal text requires solving locality and semantic mismatches: a prover might bypass a local flaw by proving an overly broad target, or validate an auto-formalized statement that drifts from the original mathematical intent. To address this, we introduce FaithSieve, a Lean-assisted framework for fine-grained evaluation of natural-language mathematical proofs. FaithSieve decomposes coarse proof steps into local reasoning units, extracts typed proof obligations, and verifies them through a formal evaluation agent. Formal validation is gated by semantic alignment scoring, so Lean evidence is incorporated only when the formal statement faithfully preserves the context, objects, and logical form of the original claim. We construct two expert-verified datasets, ProofLoc-Olympiad and ProofLoc-University, to benchmark first-error localization. On the 350-problem Olympiad dataset, FaithSieve using a GPT-5.4 backbone achieves 81.43% exact first-error accuracy, outperforming the direct-judging baseline of 72.29%. Furthermore, on the 200-problem ProofLoc-University benchmark spanning six advanced domains, FaithSieve reaches 84.5% exact accuracy, compared to 75.0% for the direct judge. Our work demonstrates that decomposing proofs into fine-grained units and grounding them with faithful formal evidence significantly improves reliable evaluation of natural-language reasoning.</p></details> | <details><summary>32 pa...</summary><p>32 pages, 3 figures; preprint with appendix and supplementary experimental details</p></details> |
| **[ReferFlow: Beyond Final-Target Localization for Complex Referring Expressions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20352v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Large Language Models have made significant progress in referring expression comprehension (REC), yet most REC benchmarks still focus on final-target localization. Experiments show that current models struggle to consistently ground intermediate visual references across successive steps. We further propose ReferFlow, which decouples interleaved text-latent trajectory generation from coordinate decoding. Specifically, Stateful Region Latent Reasoning represents each target region as an object-indexed state composed of continuous latents, with explicit visual and spatial supervision to preserve region information for subsequent grounding. Trajectory-Aware Parallel Decoding then recovers all coordinate blocks in parallel through four geometry-constrained refinement rounds, with each block conditioned on its corresponding trajectory prefix, thereby reducing within-box sequential dependencies and eliminating cross-box autoregression. We further apply Grounding-Aware GRPO to promote consistent grounding across steps and learn adaptive trajectory generation from complex REC queries. | **[Doc2Harness: Benchmarking LLMs on Long-Horizon Harness Engineering of Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20344v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agents driven by large language models are increasingly deployed on long-horizon tasks, where they use tools and modify real workspaces. In Stage 2, the frozen candidate harness runs in containers separate from the construction environment, one per test case, and is driven end to end by a scripted mock model. We score each test case using four observable channels, namely the requests sent to the model, the files on disk, the process exit code, and the command output; positive controls rule out vacuous passes. Across nineteen frontier models, each evaluated with three independent builds on 328 test cases, GPT-6 Astra achieves the highest pass rate at 53.05%, while the weakest model reaches 27.44%, a gap of roughly twenty-six percentage points. | **[Argument Graph Uncertainty: Quantifying Uncertainty from the Logical Structure of Reasoning Chains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20328v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reliable uncertainty estimation is essential for deploying large language models in high-stakes reasoning tasks, yet most existing approaches rely on expensive sampling strategies or shallow token-level signals that ignore the internal logical structure of model outputs. Crucially, our framework requires no additional calls to the evaluated model and relies entirely on small, efficient auxiliary models, producing fully interpretable, per-node uncertainty attributions at low computational cost.</p></details> |  |
| **[Beyond Static Cues: Unified Dynamic Detection of MLLM Backdoors via Dual-Flow Trajectory Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20319v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) are increasingly deployed in safety-critical domains, yet their susceptibility to backdoor attacks remains a formidable challenge. The attention branch measures how image-text attention allocation evolves during early generation, whereas the logits branch quantifies the sensitivity of the output distribution to modal perturbations through interventional ablations. We then convert these trajectories into compact sample-level metrics and normalize them against negative-control signatures so that natural perturbations are less likely to be mistaken for backdoor evidence. Code: https://github.com/kaoru0822-kitauji/EVO_Detect</p></details> |

Our formulation characterizes quantization-friendly rotations as compositions of alignment and mixing components, while recovering Hadamard matrices as a special case. Interestingly, a class of butterfly matrices belongs to this category. In the realm of LLM quantization, SpectrumQuant bridges the gap between data-agnostic structured rotations and learned rotations, providing an effective and efficient data-aware alternative for post-training quantization. Experiments on Llama and Mistral models show that SpectrumQuant is competitive with state-of-the-art rotation strategies while requiring less than one-third of the GPU memory and significantly less time than learned rotations approaches like SpinQuant and DartQuant.</p></details> |  |

For m >= 4, singleton, leave-one-out, and full-set endpoints do not generally determine the Shapley allocation; under additive set values, extra coalition evaluations are redundant. | **[Towards Efficient Threat Intelligence Instruction Tuning via Quality-Aware and Optimal Transport-Guided Data Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20284v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>High-quality data is essential for improving instruction tuning in threat intelligence analysis. | **[CSWBench: Evaluating Context-Sensitive Code-Switching in Creative Writing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20272v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated multilingual capabilities across diverse language generation and understanding tasks. | **[P2Patch: Exploit-Guided Vulnerability Repair](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20245v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models are increasingly deployed as autonomous agents for automated program repair, patching software vulnerabilities through multi-step reasoning and tool use. | **[Explain Like I'm 5 or Whatever I Choose: Evaluating the Potential for Interactive Complexity in Language Model Responses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06788v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Given the increased use of large language models (LLMs) for reading and seeking information and the varying needs and backgrounds of users, direct manipulation of text can be a valuable mechanism for end-user control. | **[TreeGraft: Adaptive Multi-Drafter Grafting for Tree-Based Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26112v3)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Speculative decoding accelerates large language model inference through a draft-then-verify paradigm. However, existing tree-structured methods use a single drafter for all drafting steps, creating a dilemma: a smaller drafter is fast but yields lower-quality trees, whereas a larger drafter improves tree quality but suffers from high latency. To address this, we propose TreeGraft, a multi-drafter framework in which drafters of different costs jointly construct a shared draft tree. TreeGraft uses the stronger drafter to rescore candidates by updating scores assigned by the weaker drafter, reselect grafting positions, and recover promising paths left unexplored. It also integrates stronger drafter expansions non-destructively, preserving existing branches that may still be accepted by the target model. Together, these designs improve the quality of the shared draft tree. To control the drafting cost, TreeGraft introduces a lightweight scheduler distilled from an offline value system to decide when to call the stronger drafter. Across 10 model pairs and 6 benchmarks, TreeGraft outperforms the better of the two fixed single-drafter endpoint strategies by 15.1% on average, reaching a maximum gain of 26.6%. Our code is available at https://github.com/fjm9933/TreeGraft.</p></details> |  |
| **[Designing Textual Interfaces To Enhance Llm Reasoning On Audio-Visual Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20085v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Automatic prompt optimization (APO) has shown strong effectiveness for text-based LLMs and LLM systems, but its application to audio-visual understanding systems requires suitable textual interfaces through which multimodal reasoning can be exposed to text-based optimizers. We compare three interface designs, including a direct inference system consisting of a single omni-modal large language model (OmniLLM), a captioner-reasoner system, and an agentic architecture in which a primary agent orchestrates subagent OmniLLMs. Experiments with Qwen3-Omni and Gemini show that direct prompt optimization provides model-dependent gains; the captioner-reasoner system and the agentic system expose more interpretable optimization interfaces but are harder to optimize, where error analysis of cases affected by optimization is conducted. Our study highlights textual interface design as an important factor in making prompt optimization effective for omni-modal reasoning systems. Our implementation details are specified in GitHub repo.</p></details> | <details><summary>Submi...</summary><p>Submitted to IEEE ICASSP 2027</p></details> |
| **[Scoping the Landscape of Large Language Model-based Affective Personal Informatics: Review, Reflection, Future Direction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20084v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Affective personal informatics (PI) involves capturing, tracking, analyzing, and reflecting on personal data to support emotional self-awareness and mental well-being. Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) are reshaping affective PI, yet a comprehensive survey of LLM-based affective PI systems is lacking. To bridge this gap, we systematically analyzed 45 papers published between January 2020 and June 2026 across four major computing and health databases, examining their macro trends, system taxonomies, and research methodologies. Our synthesis reveals an overreliance on multi-stage, text-centric pipelines, a predominance of active, screen-bound interactions, and a severe demographic skew toward young, university-campus-based cohorts in empirical evaluations. Based on these findings, we critically reflect on prevailing architectural and methodological shortcomings and outline actionable future directions for building end-to-end multimodal, low-burden, and inclusive affective PI systems.</p></details> |  |
| **[Automated Assembly Instruction Generation from CAD Models Using Grounded Large Language Models: A Human-in-the-Loop Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11896v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Assembly documentation is a downstream manufacturing artifact that is still usually authored by interpreting CAD models by hand. The proposed framework maps a STEP assembly to a typed ProductGraph intermediate representation, derives a precedence order by deterministic topological sorting, realizes each step as language conditioned only on selected graph context, attaches per-step visual documentation, and applies rule-based and model-assisted checks. PDF export remains disabled until a human reviewer resolves every quality flag. The case study establishes endto-end feasibility on a built-in six-part reference assembly: the pipeline preserves a reported assembly order and carries quantity, material, and torque into an exported manual page. Generalization and geometric validation remain open empirical questions. The contribution is an architecture that separates engineering state, deterministic reasoning, grounded language realization, verification, and human release.</p></details> | <details><summary>Withd...</summary><p>Withdrawal requested as one of the co-authors did not consent to the publication of the manuscript</p></details> |
| **[VisualWorldBench: A Fine-Grained Multi-Task Benchmark for Evaluating Visual World Knowledge in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20083v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Visual world knowledge is a core capability of multimodal large language models (MLLMs), underpinning knowledge-intensive visual question answering and fine-grained visual reasoning. However, existing benchmarks remain limited in two key respects. Second, their task construction is often insufficiently rigorous: models can answer correctly using coarse-grained cues, vague associations, or elimination strategies without truly mastering precise concepts, making it difficult to accurately assess their knowledge of fine-grained entities. We introduce VisualWorldBench, a taxonomy-grounded multi-task benchmark built on a hierarchical taxonomy of 21,291 leaf-node classes across 8 visual domains, with 32,541 evaluation instances whose ground-truth labels cover 15,479 categories across four complementary tasks: closed-set recognition, contrastive selection, precise localization, and open-world naming, covering different directions of knowledge retrieval and varying degrees of answer openness to comprehensively evaluate visual world knowledge. Most questions are constructed from taxonomically proximate categories, forcing precise discrimination among fine-grained entities. Experiments reveal that the strongest closed-source model achieves only 80.4% average accuracy, exposing capability gaps hidden under conventional benchmarks. We further construct VisualWorldBench-50K, a training resource covering 50K+ categories. Fine-tuning on VisualWorldBench-50K yields over 21 percentage points improvement on VisualWorldBench, with consistent transfer to public fine-grained recognition and visual knowledge benchmarks.</p></details> |  |
| **[Multimodal Latent Reasoning via Hierarchical Visual Cues Injection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.05359v3)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The advancement of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) has enabled impressive perception capabilities. However, their reasoning process often remains a "fast thinking" paradigm, reliant on end-to-end generation or explicit, language-centric chains of thought (CoT), which can be inefficient, verbose, and prone to hallucination. This work posits that robust reasoning should evolve within a latent space, integrating multimodal signals seamlessly. We propose multimodal latent reasoning via HIerarchical Visual cuEs injection (\emph{HIVE}), a novel framework that instills deliberate, "slow thinking" without depending on superficial textual rationales. Our method recursively extends transformer blocks, creating an internal loop for iterative reasoning refinement. Crucially, it injectively grounds this process with hierarchical visual cues from global scene context to fine-grained regional details directly into the model's latent representations. This enables the model to perform grounded, multi-step inference entirely in the aligned latent space. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that test-time scaling is effective when incorporating vision knowledge, and that integrating hierarchical information significantly enhances the model's understanding of complex scenes.</p></details> |  |
| **[Which Knowledge Should LLMs Trust? Uncovering a Generalizable Steering Direction Across Knowledge Conflicts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20067v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) frequently encounter conflicts between internal parametric knowledge and external contextual knowledge, yet the mechanisms governing this selection remain unclear. We show that the choice between context and parametric knowledge is linearly represented and can be controlled along a single direction in the model's residual stream. We then transfer a direction learned from simple factual conflicts to three more complex task families: RAG, memory, and rule following, where models must acquire information from retrieved documents, interaction histories, or explicit rules. Across four model families and multiple transfer settings, steering consistently improves the use of prompt-provided information over the unsteered model. These results suggest that the direction captures a transferable preference for external knowledge rather than a mechanism specific to factual conflicts.</p></details> |  |
| **[Human and LLM Facilitation in Collaborative Design Meetings: A Computational Behavioral Comparison](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13082v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly being explored as facilitators of group discussion and collaborative work, but the behavioral differences between LLM-generated and human facilitation remain insufficiently characterized. The analysis covers five confirmatory dimensions: contextual semantic distance, a lexical elaboration proxy, empathyand-hedge lexical density, topic divergence, and phase-vocabulary alignment. An exploratory directiveness measure failed domain validation and is not interpreted. The sampled human Project Manager turns were higher on contextual semantic distance (r = 0.644) and on empathyand-hedge lexical density (r = 0.203). The second contrast is carried mainly by hedge terms, so we read it as lexical hedging rather than psychological empathy. Llama-generated facilitation was higher on topic divergence (r = 0.703), the lexical elaboration proxy (r = 0.288; lengthconfounded), and instruction-conditioned phase-vocabulary alignment (r = 0.382). A secondary no-phase check left the Llama-higher phase contrast in place. The system prompt still requested a facilitation move, so that check is not evidence of spontaneous phase awareness. These differences should not be interpreted as a ranking of facilitation quality. The resulting behavioral profile provides a reference point for later comparisons of LLM facilitators, human facilitators, prompts, models, and collaborative settings.</p></details> | <details><summary>17 pa...</summary><p>17 pages, 7 figures, 6 tables</p></details> |
| **[Hidden in Plain Sight: Advancing Harmful Content Understanding via Perception-Aware Adversarial Self-Play](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15449v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Despite significant advances in language understanding, Large Language Models (LLMs) can still miss meaning hidden in plain sight, failing to identify harmful intent in expressions readily understood by humans. Malicious users exploit this gap by strategically disguising harmful content to evade automated moderation, posing a serious threat to online platform safety. The vulnerability reflects a fundamental cognitive asymmetry between human readers, who integrate contextual, phonetic, and visual cues to decipher the intent behind deliberate obfuscation, and text-based LLMs, which rely primarily on token sequences and have limited exposure to evolving evasions. To bridge this asymmetry, we propose PASP (Perception-Aware Adversarial Self-Play), which combines multi-perceptual views with adversarial self-play to improve understanding of harmful intent as evasions evolve. Specifically, PASP constructs textual, phonetic, and visual views of each input, preserving its original context while exposing pronunciation and glyph cues obscured in token space. During adversarial self-play, an attacker uses defender feedback to generate intent-preserving evasions, while the defender is trained using an intent-invariance reward to understand harmful intent across changing surface forms. Extensive experiments show that PASP reduces adaptive attack success rates by up to 73.71% and 74.49% on ChineseHarm-Bench and EvoHarmBench, respectively, preserves model capabilities with minimal degradation, and effectively interprets real-world evasive expressions in e-commerce.</p></details> |  |
| **[Geometric Alignment without Functional Equivalence: A Layer-wise Analysis of the Speech-Text Modality Gap](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.01502v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>End-to-end speech large language models (LLMs) often answer the same question less accurately from speech than from text, even when the spoken content matches the text prompt. Existing analyses study this modality gap through the geometry of representations and measure how closely speech representations align with text. Geometric alignment gives the gap a measurable form, but it does not show whether the model uses aligned representations in the same way. This work connects representation geometry with answer formation and asks whether geometric alignment implies functional equivalence. We compare cross-layer Centered Kernel Alignment (CKA) with layer-wise answer margins for four open-weight speech LLMs on SpeechMMLU and VoiceBench BBH. In Qwen2.5-Omni and MiniCPM-o, speech and text representations align in middle layers while their late-layer answer margins still differ, so mid-layer alignment does not imply functional equivalence. Qwen2-Audio instead shows weak alignment, which indicates a different failure mode. An attention intervention at fixed input and token count identifies a causal effect of late attention allocation on answer-code separation, a quantity distinct from answer correctness. Compressing the speech signal or its token sequence repairs a subset of the errors that occur only with speech.</p></details> | NeurIPS 2026 |
| **[Gray-Box Hallucination Detection in LLMs with Token Identities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19996v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models often produce fluent but factually incorrect output. Gray-box detectors identify such hallucinations from the token-probability statistics emitted during generation, avoiding the need for white-box access to hidden states that frontier models do not provide. These detectors, however, typically rely only on probability-derived representations and ignore the identities of the candidate tokens. They can therefore observe how uncertain the model is, but not what it is uncertain about. Consequently, they cannot distinguish between a model that is torn between "big" and "small" and one that is torn between "big" and "huge" when the two pairs receive the same probability distribution. We introduce GURI (Gray-box Uncertainty Representations with Identity), a lightweight gray-box hallucination detector that combines candidate-token probabilities with per-token vector identifiers. This exposes which alternatives the model considered while preserving the accessibility and transferability of the gray-box setting. Across three open-weight models and three question-answering benchmarks, GURI increases mean test ROC-AUC from 72.09% to 79.09% over a probability-only baseline. On the closed-source GPT-4.1 model, mean ROC-AUC increases from 67.39% to 71.84%. Among the three open-weight models, GURI outperforms transferred probability-only baseline in 17 of 18 directed cross-model transfers without fine-tuning. For the three open-weight models, cross-dataset transfer between HotpotQA and TriviaQA with the model held fixed improves mean ROC-AUC over transferred LOS-Net by 3.76 points.</p></details> |  |
| **[SoK: Grading Open-Text Legal Answers, from Law School Exams to Trustworthy Rubric Judges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19992v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Law students are examined through open-text answers: case solutions, essays and memos that apply the law to a set of facts. Such answers are expensive to grade, human graders disagree widely on them, and large language models (LLMs) now offer to grade them at scale, both in teaching and in the benchmarks that test legal AI. This paper systematizes how open-text legal answers can be evaluated. We derive eight desiderata from educational measurement and from the law that governs exams, among them validity, reliability, reasons, credit for defensible alternative solutions, robustness to gaming and human oversight. We then compare eight evaluation paradigms against them, from unstructured human grading to LLM judges with instance-specific rubrics and pairwise comparison. Three findings stand out. A rubric written for the task is the strongest lever for agreement between an LLM judge and human graders, but the reported figures answer different questions and rarely come with the human agreement they must be read against. The central validation gap lies where examination law is most demanding: defensible alternative solutions, follow-on errors, and the examiner's correction of model judgments. And rubric-centred grading is the most auditable family, because it makes the standard, its application and the final decision checkable. From this we derive the trustworthy rubric judge: an instance rubric that credits arguments and alternative paths,evidence checked in code, probes, repeated runs, calibration against independent human grades,logging, and an examiner who reads every script. We state what each safeguard rests on, what remains to be tested, and the uses the evidence supports today.</p></details> | preprint |
| **[An Empirical Study of Gemini 3 for Detecting Natural Language Test Smells in Manual Test Cases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13804v3)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Manual testing, in which testers follow natural language instructions to validate system behavior, remains essential for uncovering issues that are difficult to capture with automation. However, manual test cases often contain test smells, quality issues such as ambiguity, redundancy, or missing checks that reduce reliability, maintainability, and reproducibility. Existing detection approaches largely depend on manually engineered rules and thus struggle to generalize and scale across heterogeneous test suites. In our previous work, we assessed the feasibility of using Small Language Models (SLMs) for test smell detection by evaluating GEMMA-3-4B, LLAMA-3.2-3B, and PHI-4-14B on test steps from 143 real-world Ubuntu test cases, covering seven smell types. PHI-4-14B achieved the best performance. In this article, we investigate whether a contemporary Large Language Model (GEMINI-3-PRO-PREVIEW) available at the time of the study can identify test smells in natural language manual test cases using a prompt-based, whole-test-case analysis strategy. Unlike approaches that analyze individual test steps in isolation, our approach evaluates complete test cases, enabling the model to consider relationships and dependencies among test steps. We evaluate the approach on 100 Ubuntu test cases covering seven test smell types and compare its performance against previously evaluated SLMs, including GEMMA-3-4B, LLAMA-3.2-3B, and PHI-4-14B. Our results show that GEMINI-3-PRO-PREVIEW outperforms the SLMs, while producing actionable explanations that can help practitioners revise manual test cases for greater clarity and consistency. We also find that test smells are pervasive in practice, with nearly one detected test smell per step on average, highlighting the need for scalable and automated quality support for manual testing artifacts.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at JSERD 2026</p></details> |
| **[Temporal Adaptive Fine-Tuning for Code Comprehension](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19970v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Repository-level code comprehension question answering (QA) supports developer reasoning over functionality and usage distributed across codebases. However, repository-specialized large language models (LLMs) can become misaligned as code evolves. We study this challenge as temporal repository adaptation: acquiring new knowledge, suppressing obsolete, retaining still-valid, and preserving semantically neighboring knowledge. Existing retrieval, fine-tuning, and model-editing methods address only parts of this problem. We propose Temporal Adaptive Fine-Tuning (TAFT), a novel adaptation approach that treats repository transitions as supervision. TAFT derives temporally grounded QA data from repository transitions, pairs newly valid with obsolete knowledge through preference optimization, and replays transition-conditioned retention examples to preserve still-valid knowledge. We evaluate TAFT on an augmented temporal SWE-bench dataset comprising 255,295 QA entries across ten temporal slices, comparing deferred (Baseline), per-slice retrained (Baseline-Batched), and variants with knowledge anchors along four dimensions: updated knowledge, obsolete-answer suppression, retention, and neighbor stability. TAFT consistently outperforms both the deferred and batched baselines, with an average relative improvement of 18% in updated/suppressed knowledge accuracy, doubled retention accuracy, and roughly 1.5 times the neighbor accuracy. With directly supplied knowledge anchors, TAFT-KANT further improves mean updated/suppressed, retention, and neighbor accuracy over TAFT by 3.55%, 8.35%, and 13.00%, respectively. | **[Safe-FCL: Continual Risk Awareness and Fine-Grained Safety Aggregation for Federated Continual Learning of LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19968v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Federated fine-tuning enables large language models (LLMs) to learn collaboratively from distributed client data, but malicious client updates can compromise global model safety. Extensive experiments demonstrate that Safe-FCL outperforms current SOTA methods, improving global-model Rule and MD-Judge scores in federated continual learning by 0.41% and 0.64%, respectively, while increasing average risk recognition accuracy by 2.01%.</p></details> | <details><summary>16 pa...</summary><p>16 pages, 4 figures, 11 tables</p></details> |
| **[Taming the Online Software Engineering Tongue: Detecting and Reducing Offensive Language in Software Communities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19961v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Online Software Engineering (SE) communities such as Stack Overflow have become unwelcoming and hostile spaces, as evidenced by the presence of rude and offensive comments that are detrimental to the emotional wellbeing of participants. Also, two language-based models were developed for paraphrasing offensive to non-offensive comments. The overall utility of the OLRS-SE was evaluated using user studies with 30 participants, which showed that the developed system is easy to use and also effective in reducing offensive language. Investigating the capabilities of different large language models for offensive language reduction is a promising avenue for future research.</p></details> |  |
| **[An Experimental Study on the Functional Correctness of Network Device Configurations Produced by Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19958v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper presents a preliminary study on the efficacy of network device configuration produced by Large Language Models (LLMs). | **[P3D-Bench: Benchmarking MLLMs for Parametric 3D Generation and Structural Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.11152v4)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal large language models can write code to produce complex programs as well as use programs to do 3D modeling, which opens up a new avenue for 3D generation powered by their priors, world knowledge and reasoning. Yet existing benchmarks rarely evaluate 3D modeling through code. We introduce P3D-Bench, a benchmark for parametric 3D generation. Unlike a 3D mesh, a parametric 3D program exposes explicit dimensions, construction operations and part relations, revealing whether a model recovers a design's structure, not just its appearance. Under a unified protocol, P3D-Bench covers three task families (Text-to-3D, Image-to-3D and Assembly-3D) and scores each output for executability, geometric fidelity, topology, text-grounded constraints, multiview semantic alignment and part-level structure. First, multi-part generation is substantially more challenging than single-part modeling, with models struggling to compose individual parts into a coherent structure. Second, models can often recover the global shape and semantic identity of the target object, yet fail to reproduce the precise parametric geometry specified by the input. Project page: https://spatiaos.github.io/projects/P3D-Bench/</p></details> |
| **[When Protection Overrides Consequences: Understanding Protection Mode in Large Language Model Decision-Making](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19948v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Frontier language models excel at complex tasks yet may prioritize protection over larger physical consequences in simple, urgent decisions. At lowest effort, at least one of four responses prioritizes the subject on 34.74% of Sol prompts and over 83% of MiniMax M3 and Qwen3.5-27B prompts; none of four independent human raters does so. Controls show that choices usually track physical risk. We elicit both risks before requesting an action. Some models assess risk incorrectly; others state the correct ordering but still choose the subject. Qwen3.5-27B with thinking shows 100% correct risk ordering but 57.81% subject choice among eligible conversations on eight selected scenes. Attention audits find no stable reduction in attention to relevant facts; removing the stated risk direction reduces object-first choice in dots3-note-prev but not detectably in Qwen3.5-27B. Adding a general "people or animals before property" rule increases subject choice by 19-38 percentage points, whereas factual reminders favor the object by 0.7-5.2 points. Removing naturally generated versions of that rule lowers subject choice by 10-15 points. We also examine whether post-training introduces this pattern. Base models already show Protection Mode; post-training changes justifications more than choices.</p></details> | <details><summary>21 pa...</summary><p>21 pages. Code and data: https://github.com/AgentQ0/ProtectionBench</p></details> |
| **[MemDuet: Learning Dual-Process Memory for Long-Context Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19928v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reasoning over infinitely long documents is becoming a reality with recurrent memory agents. However, increasing context length exacerbates the trade-off between reasoning performance and inference efficiency. Inspired by Kahneman's account of fast and slow thinking, we propose MemDuet, a dual-process memory framework that learns to coordinate a small language model for fast, parallel evidence selection from raw context chunks (System 1) with a large language model for memory-update verification and memory updates in document order (System 2). We train MemDuet in three stages: supervised fine-tuning of System 1 for evidence selection, Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) of System 2 for verification and memory rewriting, and joint optimization of both policies to coordinate selection and updating for long-context reasoning. Experiments on diverse long-context reasoning benchmarks (RULER-HQA, LongBench v2, and 2WikiMultiHopQA) demonstrate a favorable accuracy-efficiency trade-off as context length grows. Averaged across evaluation settings on the three datasets, MemDuet achieves an end-to-end inference speedup of 9.74x and an output-token reduction of 73.91% relative to RL-MemAgent.</p></details> |  |
| **[Not All Criteria Matter: Importance-Aware Criteria Weighting for Rubric-Based LLM Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19915v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Rubric-based evaluation assesses the responses of large language models (LLMs) by aggregating the verification results of explicit criteria, yet not all criteria matter equally. However, existing methods commonly aggregate the verification results with uniform weighting, which assigns minor and overlapping criteria the same weight as essential ones and thus produces misleading evaluation scores. Although some methods prompt an LLM to directly assign weights, direct weighting requires a holistic judgment over all criteria that LLMs struggle to make reliably. To address these limitations, we propose Importance-Aware Criteria Weighting (ICW), which derives the weights of criteria from pairwise importance comparisons made by an LLM. Specifically, ICW transforms the holistic judgment over all criteria into a series of pairwise importance comparisons, checks and revises inconsistent comparisons, and derives the weights from the principal eigenvector of the comparison matrix. Extensive experiments demonstrate that ICW consistently outperforms uniform weighting and competitive weighting methods, and translates into better policy models in downstream reinforcement learning.</p></details> |  |
| **[When Non-Adversarial Noise Hurts Learning: Benchmarking Skill Self-Evolution in Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19910v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model agents increasingly improve by distilling experience into reusable skills, but errors in that experience can persist in learned rules and affect later tasks. Existing evaluations largely examine clean learning or perturbations encountered after skills are frozen, leaving robustness during self-evolution insufficiently understood. It spans four domains and six benchmarks, with controlled perturbations to instructions, environmental evidence, execution records, and outcome--record pairings that preserve the underlying tasks and native scoring rules. The protocol separates noise during learning from input-side noise at evaluation, testing frozen skill stores on held-out tasks. We also introduce \textsc{RSEClaw}, a method-agnostic framework supporting consistent noise injection and process diagnostics of actual exposure and skill updates. Across three methods and two backbones, noisy learning retains only $25.5\%$--$42.1\%$ of the aggregate gain achieved by clean self-evolution, depending on the noise type.
