| **[Equal CER, Unequal Damage: Severity-Weighted Character Error Rate for OCR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20461v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Character Error Rate (CER), the standard metric for OCR and vision-language models (VLMs), charges a typo as much as a wrong name. We introduce semCER, which keeps CER's alignment and denominator and charges each grapheme edit a severity from an LLM judge, with no word segmentation. Normalized CER keeps the score of a whole transcription, where it tracks readers as well as semCER or better, and semCER breaks that score down into named edits. The charges track readers' ratings edit by edit in English, Vietnamese, and Chinese and put the edits readers rate most severe near the top of a sorted list. On pairs drawn where CER and semCER disagreed, readers side with semCER more often than with normalized CER in English and Chinese, and equally often in Vietnamese. Across 16 languages, the judge charges a wrong diacritic or tone mark almost nothing in Spanish but nearly a wrong letter's severity in Telugu. Where we can count real words, a general VLM's wrong letter forms one far above chance while a classical pipeline's stays near it. We release the 61 million judged edits, the aggregated reader ratings, and the distilled scorer at https://github.com/nguyenx2t/semcer.</p></details> | <details><summary>26 pa...</summary><p>26 pages, 7 figures, 14 tables. Under review at ACL Rolling Review</p></details> |

Reinforcement learning can improve reasoning via reward signals, but it remains unclear whether these signals must come from outside the model or can be derived from the model itself. We compare external rewards, which rely on an outside verifier (rule-based format and answer-correctness checks, or an LLM-judge rubric), with an internal attention-based reward computed from the model's own attention over image regions, requiring no labels or judge. We find that external rewards drive most of the accuracy gains, while preserving more coherent reasoning when rewarded for reasoning tokens. The internal attention-based reward, provides an annotation-free grounding signal that improves spatial-relation reasoning and is complementary to external rewards: adding it to format and accuracy rewards, at no additional annotation cost, improves average accuracy on Molmo~2 while generalizing to out-of-distribution tasks. Our results suggest that internal attention rewards can supplement external supervision and lead to improved physical reasoning.</p></details> |  |

Experiments show clear improvements on remote sensing, while fashion results reveal strong gains in classification and particularly strong retrieval improvements for difficult queries. To bridge this gap and facilitate universal positioning, we introduce CORE, the first million-scale dataset dedicated to global CMGL. CORE comprises 1,034,786 cross-view images sampled from 225 distinct geographic regions across six continents, offering an unprecedented variety of perspectives in varying environmental conditions and urban layouts. We leverage the zero-shot reasoning of Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs) to synthesize high-quality scene descriptions rich in discriminative cues. Furthermore, we propose a physical-law-aware network (PLANET) for cross-modal geo-localization. PLANET introduces a novel contrastive learning paradigm to guide textual representations in capturing the intrinsic physical signatures of satellite imagery. | **[RealityBench: Simulated Environments to Evaluate a Vision Language Model's Ability to Document Human Physical Work](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14397v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Augmenting worker capabilities in the physical world through guidance has been a decades-long goal for AI agents. Here, we model real-world physical work in Blender across eleven environments, incorporating variants in both image acceptability and documentation. The acceptability task asks whether a particular view would be suitable proof-of-work and documentation asks whether an agent can understand the task-relevant state of the environment from that view. Across 26 vision-language models, we find that top models largely solve documentation when the answer is text in frame, but are weak at reading measurements and at judging small parts and spatial relations, such as whether a tape measure leans. Acceptability is harder to pin down because human raters disagree with each other. Measured against a scorer that agrees with the raters' majority about as often as a single rater does, the best models cannot be distinguished from a single non-author rater, at a cost within the range of a human pass. | **[Align Where the Words Look: Cross-Attention-Guided Patch Alignment with Contrastive and Transport Regularization for Bengali Captioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18369v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Adapting vision-language models in low-resource languages remains challenging, as they often produce fluent text about the wrong objects. | **[Integrating Simulation and Chain-of-thought Reasoning in Multimodal-Language Models For Physical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19441v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal language models often need to reason about the physical dynamics of the world in order to answer questions appropriately. Our work addresses this by systematically comparing simulation and language reasoning across a variety of multimodal tasks involving physical reasoning, such as answering questions about fluid dynamics and elasticity. We draw a parallel with this to resource-rational theories of human cognition, which propose that people optimally trade off simulation and reasoning depending on task demands. We present a novel cognitively-inspired hybrid model which optimally trades off simulation and reasoning to improve model performance and computational efficiency. Our model is able to decide the appropriate method to solve a physical reasoning problem at test time, and finds an optimal balance between accuracy and cost. Further, our model can be trained on a smaller vision-language model that is only 4B parameters and deployed to improve performance on much bigger base models with $>$3T parameters like Gemini 3 Pro.</p></details> |  |
| **[VideoHarness-SRSI: Structured Recursive Self-Improvement for Video Understanding Harness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19424v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM system capabilities depend not only on model parameters but also on the executable harnesses that organize information acquisition, tool use, reasoning, and memory. Analyzing over 4,000 evolved candidates, we find it competitive with hand-engineered systems but identify three interconnected failure modes: validation overfitting, search-budget dilution, and the early-experience trap. We trace all three to a common root: the same imperfect validation evidence drives both harness revisions and the proposer's search strategy. We therefore propose VideoHarness-SRSI, an evidence-governed framework in which every search decision rests on evidence that is transferable, sufficient, and gathered before the search commits. | **[LAMP: 3D Source-Aware Lighting Estimation at Multiple Points](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19266v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Existing illumination estimation methods primarily recover lighting distributions for rendering, but typically leave the contributions of individual physical light sources at specific scene locations implicit. | **[CLASHarness: Benchmarking Multi-Agent Conflict in Computer Use via a Controllable and Verifiable Generation Pipeline](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19128v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision-language models (VLMs) are increasingly deployed in multi-agent systems to automate complex computer-use workflows, yet existing benchmarks mostly assume consistent, cooperative environments. | **[The Value of Being Wrong: Self-Mined Visual In-Context Learning from Errors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16977v1)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Visual in-context learning (ICL) enables vision-language models (VLMs) to adapt at test time via demonstration examples, but its effectiveness depends critically on the examples selected. | **[What Drives Test-Time Adaptation for CLIP? | **[Co-Evolving Acting and Forecasting for Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20597v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Training language agents is bottlenecked by the high cost of long-horizon rollouts, yet standard agentic reinforcement learning (RL) learns only from a terminal scalar reward, leaving the supervision in intermediate steps largely unused. Prior work exploits this supervision through value targets that estimate per-step success probabilities or observation targets that fit environment response. Both are hard to construct well: (1) value targets come from the same single scalar signal for each step in the trajectory, and (2) observation targets, such as web pages and terminal logs, are long and noisy and reach only one step ahead. We propose \name, which adds a third kind of multi-step action targets. On successful on-policy trajectories, \name trains the policy backbone to generate the action sequences that follows each history. Each action is also supervised when forecast from the preceding histories, forming a temporal ensemble that the shared backbone absorbs during training, and this enables acting and forecasting to co-evolve. The policy still acts one step at a time at inference, with no overhead. Across five benchmarks (ALFWorld, ScienceWorld, WebShop, $τ^2$-bench, and AppWorld), \name (1) achieves stronger aggregate performance than GRPO and RL baselines with predictive auxiliary targets across simulated environments, while improving overall performance on both realistic applications; (2) delivers up to $1.9\times$ and $2.0\times$ higher sample efficiency than ECHO and GRPO, respectively, on ScienceWorld; and (3) achieves these gains with a single policy backbone, without additional models, separate pretraining, or inference overhead. The codebase is available at https://github.com/ulab-uiuc/CoPE.</p></details> |  |
| **[Price of Anarchy of First-Price Auctions in Agentic Markets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20587v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>An agentic market is a decentralized serving-time market where AI agents bid to claim each user prompt. An agent's bid is based on its expected value for the prompt, which is the expectation of the reward for its response minus its inference costs. First-price auctions (FPAs), in which the highest bidder pays its bid and receives the prompt, are a natural way to allocate prompts in such a market, since they are simple and transparent to run, and do not require central knowledge of private capabilities and costs. However, FPAs are not truthful: participants may bid less than their true value in the hope of a larger profit, and the prompt may then be allocated to someone other than the bidder who values it the most. This welfare loss is quantified by the $\textit{price-of-anarchy}$ (PoA). In this paper, we argue that agentic markets are likely to possess features that reduce the price-of-anarchy. The first is a $\textit{competitive floor}$, which occurs when multiple bidders usually value the prompt above some baseline share of the optimal welfare (e.g., because the best models are often distilled into cheaper alternatives, which perform similarly). We show that competition among them then limits the welfare loss, which can be driven to zero as the floor rises. The second feature is $\textit{near-independence}$ of values after conditioning on the prompt, which occurs when most of the predictable variation in the expected value is determined by the difficulty of the prompt. As the value distribution approaches conditional independence, we can derive an efficiency guarantee that approaches a factor of $1 - 1/e^2$ of the optimal welfare. Thus, both features give rise to welfare lower bounds that are significantly better than the well-known general case of $1 - 1/e$. We validate these results empirically using auctions with strategic bidders simulated on the EmbedLLM prompt routing dataset.</p></details> | 40 pages |
| **[One Task, Many Interfaces: Simple Cross-Interface Consistency Training Improves LLM Agent Robustness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20576v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Post-training binds the competence of an LLM agent to the interface it was trained on. Semantics-preserving perturbations of that interface, such as renaming an action or changing the notation, leave the underlying task unchanged yet make the agent worse at it. Scoring each sample independently means interface randomization only broadens this dependence from a single interface to the training interface distribution, so out-of-distribution perturbations still degrade the agent. We propose Cross-Interface Policy Regularization (CIPR), a self-supervised objective that replaces action names with uninformative identifiers and presents each training sample under two interfaces in which equivalent actions are identified by construction. CIPR penalizes the KL divergence between the action distributions induced under the two interfaces, so the policy behaves consistently across them. We evaluate CIPR in both supervised and reinforcement-learning post-training, where it improves robustness. On agentic function calling, held-out interface perturbations reduce the accuracy of a post-trained agent by nearly 25%, and CIPR recovers 10.5% while leaving accuracy on the unchanged interface intact.</p></details> |  |
| **[Any Time, Any Space: A World-State-Centric Framework for Agentic Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20571v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-form agentic video generation must turn a screenplay into shots that remain spatially and temporally consistent across nonconsecutive requests. Passing prompts or recent frames between shots does not provide a shared spatial frame for revisiting a scene or a time-indexed record for replaying an earlier event. We present Any Time, Any Space (ATAS), which compiles a screenplay into shot requests with explicit location, camera, and event-time references. Before generation, it retrieves relevant evidence from a scene memory of registered static geometry and an event memory of dynamic observations indexed by scene, event, and time. After generation, ATAS reconstructs and registers the clip. It updates memory only when review and state checks accept the observation, while film delivery remains independent of memory admission. Across four video generators, ATAS improves all reported cross-shot consistency measures and achieves the highest aggregate benchmark score for each backbone. It also outperforms the compared agents and 3D-guided methods on target-image spatial revisit, leads three event-recapture criteria, and receives the highest human ratings on all six criteria. The video results and code are available on our project page: https://lhmd.top/atas/.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project page: https://lhmd.top/atas/, Code: https://github.com/ziplab/ATAS</p></details> |
| **[PhySGA: A Physics-Space Grounded Agentic Framework for Generalizable and Interactive 3D Composition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20560v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>3D object composition is pivotal to digital content creation and robot simulation, while also serving as a critical testbed for physical intelligence in foundation models. Previous methods either leverage large language models to directly predict object coordinates, or utilize generative diffusion priors to synthesize composite images for visual alignment, both of which rely on static priors in digital spaces. However, these methods consistently struggle with complex compositions such as precise hanging, gravity-driven settling and hidden containment, as these tasks require interactive construction processes involving global planning, fine-grained alignment, and physical simulation. To address these challenges, we propose PhySGA, a Physics-Space Grounded Agentic Framework for 3D composition via agent exploration and physical simulation, enabling the universal interactive assembly of rigid, articulated, and deformable objects. Directly applying vanilla LLMs or VLMs to this task proves insufficient, as current models inherently lack grounded physical understanding and interactive construction experience. To overcome this limitation, we design a collaborative feedforward-feedback architecture: an Assembly Agent focuses on geometric and kinematic reasoning for global step planning and coarse adjustment, while a Critic Agent reasons over contact dynamics for simulator state diagnosis and fine-grained closed-loop correction. Furthermore, Action Routers map semantic planning and analysis onto executable physical primitives within the simulator, while a task-specific Self-Evolving Skill Library distills historical trial-and-error feedback into reusable experiences, boosting success rates and minimizing exploration attempts. Extensive experiments within Isaac Sim demonstrate that PhySGA significantly surpasses existing methods in relation fidelity, physical plausibility, and visual quality.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project Page: https://agenticei.github.io/PhySGA-page/</p></details> |
| **[EmbodiedEvo: Self-Evolving Coding Agent For Physical Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18867v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Coding agents can construct executable programs and revise them through feedback, offering a foundation for embodied control without target-task fine-tuning. Through a programmatic robot interface, the agent observes the scene, executes control programs, and adapts them to the resulting physical state, serving as the closed-loop policy. However, this within-episode adaptation does not automatically preserve experience across scenes. Our trajectory analysis shows that agents spend interaction and inference budgets establishing effective operations and identifying their preconditions, while fresh agents cannot reuse these discoveries. This motivates EmbodiedEvo, a self-evolving coding agent that distills physical feedback into reusable code Skills and diagnostic Lessons. Experience is acquired on one Playground scene per task, then frozen for independent agents in unseen scenes, without updating model weights. On RoboDojo-Sim, EmbodiedEvo achieves an average score of 44.48 and a success rate of 37.82\%, surpassing all officially verified results. Experience from stronger models also improves weaker executors: Astra's experience raises Sol and Luna's success rates by 8.86 and 5.50 percentage points over no experience. | **[MOCHA: Multi-Objective Chebyshev Annealing for Agent Skill Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.19330v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents organize behavior through skills -- structured natural-language specifications that govern how an agent reasons, retrieves, and responds. Unlike monolithic prompts, skills are multi-field artifacts with platform limits: descriptions are indexed for routing, instruction bodies are loaded into a shared context window, and every extra line costs the agent context it could spend elsewhere. Skill optimization is therefore inherently multi-objective: a skill should be accurate and economical at once. Existing prompt optimizers collapse these goals into a single score, and we find that this makes them stall: in most of their runs, 1000 rollouts yield zero progress over the seed skill. We introduce MOCHA (Multi-Objective CHebyshev Annealing), which selects skills to improve by Chebyshev scalarization and accepts improvements that expand the Pareto front, annealing from exploration to exploitation. Chebyshev scalarization scores a skill by its largest weighted shortfall from a perfect skill; with random weights, it can select any skill on the Pareto front for improvement, including trade-offs that an average would never favor. Across six diverse skills and two models, MOCHA escapes the seed in nearly every run, achieving 7.9% higher mean accuracy than the strongest baseline (up to 23.7% on DebugBench with GPT 5.5 and 14.9% on FEVER with Claude Haiku 4.5) while discovering Pareto fronts with the highest hypervolume and nearly twice as many Pareto-optimal skills.</p></details> | <details><summary>Prepr...</summary><p>Preprint. 24 pages, 20 figures, 6 tables</p></details> |
| **[SkillCommit: Evolving Agent Skills through Behaviorally Validated Scope Expansion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15165v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents can continually improve without parameter updates by converting historical experience into reusable procedural knowledge. | **[Agentic AI with Structured CoT for Enhancing AI's Spatial Intelligence: Visualization and Reasoning of Rotation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04188v3)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent studies show that artificial intelligence (AI) with language and vision capabilities still experiences limitations in spatial reasoning. | **[The Wealth of Agents: Scaling Agentic AI Across Developers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20518v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Today's agentic systems are typically designed, built, and deployed by single developers with full control over all participating agents. A shared probabilistic belief state couples intervention simulation, observation selection, and outcome assimilation, enabling the digital twin to continuously refine its decision-relevant representations through externally verified evidence. We evaluate NeuronSifter in Alzheimer's disease intervention scenarios using mechanistic neuronal microenvironment simulations and retrospective clinical endpoint references. The evaluation framework examines adaptive evidence acquisition, uncertainty-aware intervention planning, and robustness under model discrepancy through controlled environments and independent comparisons. NeuronSifter demonstrates how an AI system can actively identify missing evidence, refine biological hypotheses, and optimize intervention decisions under limited experimental budgets. Code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/NeuronSifter.</p></details> | <details><summary>The s...</summary><p>The submission was made without obtaining authorization from all co-authors</p></details> |

NeuronDiscover is an Agent-in-Twin framework whose shared, mechanism-grounded World Action Model (WAM) couples prediction, intervention proposals, and observation design; independently adjudicated outcomes revise a scoped Mechanism--Intervention--Observation--Outcome (MIOY) graph, whose supported relations compile into executable programs carrying discrepancy-adjusted acceptance bounds. Joint mechanism--discrepancy acquisition resolves 3.8 relations versus 2.9 for plug-in expected information gain; discrepancy-adjusted verification lowers accepted-program failure from 15% to 9% at 60% acceptance coverage; and transfer to the recordings yields 1.94 versus 1.53 relations per assigned world. | **[What and Where in Identifying and Quantifying Conventions in Humans and AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20205v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cooperative success rests in part on implicit communication. Partners converge on conventions: regularities that give an action meaning beyond its direct effect. Yet conventions are usually observed only indirectly: a score drop when partners are swapped shows that a convention existed, but not what it was or where it was used. In the card game Hanabi, the exact probability that a play fails can be computed from the hints received and the cards in view. Players who use conventions succeed more often than this prediction allows; the resulting margin is the convention gap. Off-belief learning provides a hierarchy of agents (levels 1-5) whose first level uses no conventions by construction and whose later levels use progressively more. For humans and these agents on one scale, we ask what the conventions are and where they are used. On what: in two independently collected corpora of human games, humans show a larger gap than every agent level. On the conventions both populations use, however, humans do no better than level 2. The human advantage is concentrated in a situation that no agent level learns to use: hints that point to a card other than the ones they touch. On where: conventions depend on what the player already knows about the card, not on how recently it was hinted, and they are used most in the mid-game; humans use them earlier and later in the game than the agents. In interventions on the agents, blocking a single high-gap situation costs little, because meaning reroutes to other situations, but blocking several together costs far more than the sum of the single costs. Thus, the convention gap turns conventions from an inferred cause of score loss into a measurable, comparable, and causally testable object.</p></details> | <details><summary>32 pa...</summary><p>32 pages, 12 figures, 6 tables</p></details> |

| **[TimeWarp: Evaluating Web Agents by Revisiting the Past](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.04949v4)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As web agents close the gap with humans on benchmarks, one question arises: Do today's agents perform just as well on tomorrow's web? | **[MolHop: Scaffold-Hopping Molecular Design with Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19920v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Molecular design is often formulated as an optimization problem over existing molecular spaces. | **[When Non-Adversarial Noise Hurts Learning: Benchmarking Skill Self-Evolution in Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19910v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model agents increasingly improve by distilling experience into reusable skills, but errors in that experience can persist in learned rules and affect later tasks. SEDM exceeds the strongest evaluated baseline on LoCoMo open-domain questions by 15.1 F1 points and outperforms G-Memory on FEVER and HotpotQA with 16-32% fewer prompt tokens. In a controlled HotpotQA study, CMG achieves success comparable to always using memory with 18% fewer agent input tokens. Within the full SEDM pipeline on LoCoMo, CMG outperforms both always-use and no-memory policies for two SEDM memories while using 19-31% fewer input tokens than always-use. These results show that selective use can recover value from shared memories that hurt performance when injected indiscriminately.</p></details> |  |
| **[J-tau: A Fully Localized Japanese Benchmark for Tool-Agent-User Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19638v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We present J-tau, the first publicly available Japanese benchmark for evaluating tool-using LLM agents in interactive customer-support scenarios. While tau-bench is widely used to evaluate LLM agents, it is English-centric and cannot evaluate agents in other languages. Existing multilingual extensions offer no public Japanese version and rely primarily on machine translation, leaving locale-specific entities such as addresses and currencies inconsistent with the target region. To enable reliable evaluation in Japanese, we carefully localize tau-bench through machine translation, entity adaptation, and human review. During this process, we also uncover and fix two flaws in the original tau-bench: invalid tasks and a score aggregation error that inflates certain models' results. Experiments show that J-tau yields fewer evaluation errors than a naively machine-translated counterpart and that our fixes resolve both flaws.</p></details> |  |
| **[Beyond Perfect Teammates: Multi-Agent Collaboration with Unreliable Colleagues](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16117v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM-based multi-agent collaboration is increasingly used to solve complex tasks by dividing work among specialized agents and integrating complementary expertise. Yet existing systems and evaluations largely rest on an implicit perfect-colleague assumption: collaborators provide correct and complete information and respond promptly to requests. In realistic teamwork, however, collaborators may return information that does not answer the intended question, provide insufficient detail to support a decision, or fail to respond to a request. To separate task-solving competence from the ability to recover an unreliable evidence-gathering process, we introduce the Unreliable Colleagues Benchmark (UnCoBench), which organizes behavioral failures through Misaligned, Incomplete, and Missed settings. The main agent need to verify contextual correctness, diagnose information gap and track unresolved dependencies in order to obtain the correct evidence for solving the task. We also introduce a verifiable and scalable automated generation pipeline that pairs LLM-based scenario generation with executable checks of answer uniqueness and role necessity. Results across ten models show that similar reliable-setting performance can result in different robustness to unreliable evidence. Trace analysis reveals frequent omissions of corrective follow-up, and the recovery behavior involves inefficient waiting and redundant queries. These findings show that robust collaboration requires not only sound reasoning, but also targeted action to diagnose evidence failures, recover relevant information, and integrate it into decisions. Our data and source code are available at https://github.com/iNLP-Lab/UnCoBench.</p></details> | <details><summary>24 pa...</summary><p>24 pages, 8 figures, 8 tables</p></details> |
| **[F2A-ObjectNav: An Adaptive Agentic Architecture for Object-Goal Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19618v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Object-goal navigation requires an embodied agent to explore an unfamiliar environment, identify a target object, navigate toward candidate instances, and reliably determine whether the observed object satisfies the mission goal. This work investigates this problem through a two-stage development process. In Stage I, a fixed modular framework is used to examine combinations of exploration, object detection, and target validation methods. The results reveal clear trade-offs among navigation performance, validation reliability, execution time, and model-related cost, with no single fixed configuration remaining preferable across all evaluated conditions. These observations motivate F2A-ObjectNav (Fixed-to-Adaptive Object-Goal Navigation), an adaptive agentic architecture in which a rule-based Supervisor coordinates specialized components for navigation and exploration, object detection, target validation, semantic guidance, and Mission Memory. The architecture uses event-driven recovery, strategy revision, candidate management, and conditional semantic guidance while retaining lightweight execution during normal operation. F2A-ObjectNav is evaluated over 25 episodes in each of AI2-THOR, RoboTHOR, and ProcTHOR, achieving success rate (SR) / success-weighted path length (SPL) values of 0.84/0.52, 0.40/0.22, and 0.44/0.37, respectively. In addition to task-level metrics, architecture-specific behavioral metrics are used to characterize semantic-guidance usage, strategy switching, recovery activation, memory-supported behavior, and decision stability. The results show different patterns of adaptive intervention across the three environments, with higher intervention frequencies observed in RoboTHOR and ProcTHOR than in AI2-THOR.</p></details> |  |
| **[Divergent Paths: Learning an Outcome-Predictive Representation of LLM Agent Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19614v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In this work, we reframe agentic trajectories as paths through a shared embedding space of actions and environmental observations. We show that successful and failed rollouts of the same task separate in this space, with simple per-task discriminators predicting held-out rollout outcomes at up to 0.95 AUROC across three agent benchmarks and three policy models. To exploit this signal across tasks, we introduce Precog-RM, a transformer-based model that meta-learns a representation in which outcome prediction can be performed in a few-shot setting. We show that Precog-RM generalizes to unseen tasks and models, and gives confident predictions of trajectory success long before termination. Deployed as an early stopping controller for Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) on Tau2-Bench, Precog-RM cuts training FLOPs by up to 47.3\% while maintaining performance comparable to baseline GRPO.</p></details> | <details><summary>9 pag...</summary><p>9 pages, 8 pages appendix</p></details> |
